A · correct — ALTER changes an existing relation definition and supports adding a column.

B · incorrect — MODIFY alone is not the general command for adding attributes to an existing relation.

C · incorrect — TAILOR is not an SQL schema-change command established here.

D · incorrect — ELIMINATE is not the SQL command for adding a relation attribute.

Question 113

Which keyword pair changes a table definition to remove a specified column, rather than changing or deleting row values? Treat the comma in an option as separating keyword names.

  • A. UPDATE TABLE
  • B. TRUNCATE COLUMN
  • C. ALTER, DROP
  • D. DELETE COLUMN

Adapted from

  • Database Fundamentals practice questions · Question 48

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Distinguish table definition changes from changes to stored rows.

Study-guide sections

FDB30 · CREATE and ALTER tables

FDB26 · SQL command categories and exact scope

FDB29 · INSERT, UPDATE and DELETE

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

FDB30 · CREATE and ALTER tables

FDB26 · SQL command categories and exact scope

FDB29 · INSERT, UPDATE and DELETE

Correct answer: C — ALTER, DROP

Removing a column changes the table's definition. The appropriate form is ALTER TABLE followed by DROP COLUMN, so the keyword pair represented by C is correct. Its comma separates the two keyword names rather than belonging in executable SQL. UPDATE changes stored row values, DELETE removes rows, and TRUNCATE empties a table while retaining its definition; none of those operations removes the specified column definition.

Evidence in the complete study note

ALTER TABLE Employee ADD COLUMN Email VARCHAR(120); adds a column in PostgreSQL-style syntax. ALTER TABLE Employee DROP COLUMN Email; removes it. ALTER TABLE Employee ADD CONSTRAINT uq_email UNIQUE (Email); adds a named constraint. Use ALTER rather than DALTER, and do not separate column_name and column_type with a comma; validate the resulting SQL before execution.FDB30
DELETE removes rows and keeps the table; DROP TABLE removes the table object; ALTER changes its definition.FDB26
TRUNCATE empties a table using implementation-specific mechanisms.FDB26
UPDATE Book SET Price = 600 WHERE ID = 'B001'; changes matching rows. UPDATE without WHERE targets every row.FDB29

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — Incorrect: UPDATE changes row values rather than removing a column definition.

B · incorrect — Incorrect: TRUNCATE empties a table; TRUNCATE COLUMN is not the column-removal form.

C · correct — Correct: ALTER TABLE ... DROP COLUMN removes a column definition.

D · incorrect — Incorrect: DELETE removes rows, not a column from the schema.

Question 114

Which one of these is used with SELECT clause to fetch all columns from a table?

  • A. ALL
  • B. *
  • C. DISTINCT
  • D. AS

Source

  • Database Fundamentals practice questions · Question 49

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Trace how SELECT forms its result columns and rows.

Study-guide sections

FDB27 · SELECT and its result columns

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

FDB27 · SELECT and its result columns

Correct answer: B — The asterisk (*).

FDB27 explicitly says SELECT * selects all columns. ALL and DISTINCT instead govern duplicate result rows, so neither means every column. AS introduces an alias. A query still needs the appropriate FROM source for table columns; the question asks only which SELECT-list symbol requests them all.

Evidence in the complete study note

SELECT Name,Address FROM Customer; produces only those mailing-label fields. SELECT * selects all columns; DISTINCT removes duplicate result rows; AS supplies an alias; ALL permits duplicates and does not mean all columns. SQL normally preserves duplicates without DISTINCT. A query reading table columns needs an appropriate FROM source. A literal-only query such as SELECT 1 may legally omit FROM.FDB27
WHERE alone is a clause rather than a complete read-only statement, so it cannot be compared as an equivalent executable command.FDB27
Typical written clause order is SELECT … FROM … WHERE … GROUP BY … HAVING … ORDER BY ….FDB27

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — ALL permits duplicate result rows and does not expand the result to every column.

B · correct — The wildcard * requests every column from the selected table source.

C · incorrect — DISTINCT removes duplicate result rows rather than selecting all available columns.

D · incorrect — AS supplies an alias for a result expression or source, not a wildcard column list.

Question 115

A parent table has a DATE column, a nullable data column and a primary-key column referenced by existing child rows with ON UPDATE RESTRICT. Which column's change to a different otherwise valid value is blocked specifically because the old key is still referenced? No other constraints or triggers block changes.

  • A. DATE type columns in the table
  • B. Columns which allows NULL values in the table
  • C. A primary key column which also serves as foreign key reference in another table
  • D. All of the above

Adapted from

  • Database Fundamentals practice questions · Question 50

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Read each SQL constraint's scope and enforcement rule.

Study-guide sections

FDB16 · Write and enforce SQL constraints

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

FDB16 · Write and enforce SQL constraints

Correct answer: C — C. A primary key column which also serves as foreign key reference in another table

The stated restrictive action prevents changing the parent key while existing children still refer to its old value. This is a consequence of the particular reference state and action, rather than inherent immutability of every primary key. DATE and nullable columns can accept otherwise valid changes. Therefore only the referenced primary-key column has the specified update block.

Evidence in the complete study note

PRIMARY KEY implies uniqueness and non-NULL. UNIQUE does not generally imply NOT NULL; NULL treatment differs by DBMS. A CHECK can pass when its result is unknown, so add NOT NULL if missing values must be forbidden. Parent updates/deletes may use NO ACTION/RESTRICT, CASCADE or SET NULL. A referenced key is not inherently unchangeable: legality depends on matching children and the declared action.FDB16

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — DATE values can be changed to other permitted dates.

B · incorrect — Allowing NULL does not itself block valid updates.

C · correct — Existing child references plus ON UPDATE RESTRICT block this key change.

D · incorrect — The date and nullable columns are not blocked under the given conditions.

Question 116

Using the course classification that includes SELECT in DML, which listed language terms can describe application requests to retrieve database data? Select all that apply.

  • A. DML
  • B. DDL
  • C. Query language
  • D. All of the Mentioned

Adapted from

  • Database Fundamentals practice questions · Question 51

Classify each SQL command by the kind of change it performs.

Study-guide sections

FDB26 · SQL command categories and exact scope

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

FDB26 · SQL command categories and exact scope

Correct answers (select all): A, C — A. DML; C. Query language

A query language expresses requests for data. The course also includes SELECT retrieval within DML, alongside insertion, update and deletion. Both terms can therefore describe the application's retrieval request at different levels of terminology. DDL changes structural definitions instead of expressing the requested retrieval. Since DDL does not fit, the aggregate all-of-the-mentioned choice is also false.

Evidence in the complete study note

DDL changes structure: CREATE, ALTER, DROP, commonly RENAME and TRUNCATE in textbook classifications. DML retrieves or changes data: SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE. Some texts separate SELECT as DQL. DCL manages privileges through GRANT and REVOKE. TCL includes COMMIT, ROLLBACK and SAVEPOINT. Classify a statement by its effect, not its length.FDB26

Choice by choice

A · correct — DML includes retrieval with SELECT in the note's classification.

B · incorrect — DDL changes schema structure rather than expressing ordinary data retrieval requests.

C · correct — A query language expresses requests for data, directly matching the stem.

D · incorrect — DDL does not fit the retrieval role, so the inclusive choice is false.

Question 118

In the course's SQL command taxonomy, how is TRUNCATE classified?

  • A. DDL
  • B. DML
  • C. DCL
  • D. TCL

Adapted from

  • Database Fundamentals practice questions · Question 53

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Classify each SQL command by the kind of change it performs.

Study-guide sections

FDB26 · SQL command categories and exact scope

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

FDB26 · SQL command categories and exact scope

Correct answer: A — DDL

The course groups TRUNCATE with DDL commands. It empties a table through mechanisms that depend on the implementation, but that fact does not turn it into a privilege-management or transaction-boundary command. DML covers commands such as INSERT, UPDATE and DELETE; DCL covers GRANT and REVOKE; TCL covers COMMIT, ROLLBACK and SAVEPOINT. The requested classification is the course convention, not a claim that TRUNCATE has identical recovery behavior in every DBMS.

Evidence in the complete study note

DDL changes structure: CREATE, ALTER, DROP, commonly RENAME and TRUNCATE in textbook classifications. DML retrieves or changes data: SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE. Some texts separate SELECT as DQL. DCL manages privileges through GRANT and REVOKE. TCL includes COMMIT, ROLLBACK and SAVEPOINT. Classify a statement by its effect, not its length.FDB26

Choice by choice

A · correct — Correct: the course's textbook classification includes TRUNCATE under DDL.

B · incorrect — Incorrect: DML includes row operations such as INSERT, UPDATE and DELETE; TRUNCATE is assigned to DDL in this taxonomy.

C · incorrect — Incorrect: DCL manages privileges through commands such as GRANT and REVOKE.

D · incorrect — Incorrect: TCL controls transaction boundaries or rollback points, unlike TRUNCATE.

Question 119

A type of query that is placed within a WHERE or HAVING clause of another query is called

  • A. Master query
  • B. Sub query
  • C. Super query
  • D. Multi-query

Source

  • Database Fundamentals practice questions · Question 54

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Study-guide sections

FDB33 · Subqueries and views

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

FDB33 · Subqueries and views

Correct answer: B — Sub query

The optimization section identifies nested queries as separate query blocks, and the view example places a SELECT inside an outer WHERE. Subquery is the offered name matching this inner/nested query; WHERE or HAVING placement does not define every possible subquery location.

Evidence in the complete study note

A subquery is a SELECT nested inside another statement. It may appear in WHERE, HAVING or FROM, not only the first two. IN tests membership; EXISTS tests whether at least one qualifying row exists. Correlated subqueries refer to the outer row. A scalar subquery must produce an appropriate single value. Beware NOT IN when its subquery can return NULL; NOT EXISTS often expresses the intended absence more safely.FDB33
CREATE VIEW CSStudent AS SELECT StudentID,Name FROM Student WHERE Major = 'CS'; stores a query definition and presents a virtual table.FDB33
An ordinary view does not necessarily store its result; a materialized view does.FDB33

C · incorrect — Super query does not name the inner block. (PDF pp. 34, 58.)

D · incorrect — Multi-query does not identify the nesting relationship. (PDF pp. 34, 58.)

Question 122

FDB34 · Set operations and type conversion in SQL

CAST is the standard expression form that requests conversion of a value to a target type. Question 123

Which SQL keyword combines the rows returned by two compatible SELECT statements into one result set, removing duplicates by default?

  • A. COMBINE
  • B. CONCAT
  • C. JOIN
  • D. UNION

Adapted from

  • Database Fundamentals practice questions · Question 59

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Check set membership, duplicates and compatible result types.

Study-guide sections

FDB34 · Set operations and type conversion in SQL

FDB31 · Join behavior and preservation

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

FDB34 · Set operations and type conversion in SQL

FDB31 · Join behavior and preservation

Correct answer: D — UNION

UNION combines the rows of compatible query results and removes duplicates by default. It is written between the SELECT statements. JOIN instead combines input rows through matching or product behavior within a query, rather than taking the set union of completed results. CONCAT concerns concatenation of values, and COMBINE does not form the required SQL set-operation syntax. D is therefore the unique correct selection for this result-set operation.

Evidence in the complete study note

SELECT ID FROM A UNION SELECT ID FROM B; combines compatible result columns and removes duplicate rows by default. UNION ALL retains duplicates. Explicit UNION DISTINCT is accepted in some dialects, including PostgreSQL; it is not a separate algebra rule. INTERSECT returns common rows; EXCEPT returns first-result rows absent from the second. Inputs need the same column count and compatible corresponding types.FDB34

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — Incorrect: COMBINE is not the UNION set-operation keyword required between these SELECT statements.

B · incorrect — Incorrect: concatenating values does not perform the requested union of query-result rows.

C · incorrect — Incorrect: JOIN forms combinations of input rows rather than this union of compatible SELECT results.

D · correct — Correct: UNION combines compatible query results and removes duplicates by default.

FDB34 · Set operations and type conversion in SQL

UNION eliminates duplicate result rows by default. In the stated dialect, DISTINCT explicitly requests that same behavior, while ALL retains duplicates. SELECT ID FROM A UNION SELECT ID FROM B; combines compatible result columns and removes duplicate rows by default. UNION ALL retains duplicates. FDB19 · Insertion, deletion and update anomalies

Normalization separates facts according to their determinants, reducing avoidable repetition and associated anomalies. It does not require seeing bad current data: declared dependencies suffice. Question 127

In the course terminology, which labels can name a drawing of relational tables and their primary-key/foreign-key references? Select all that apply. ER Diagram means a conceptual entity/relationship drawing; Relational Schema means a textual table definition here.

  • A. ER Diagram
  • B. Database Diagram
  • C. Schema Diagram
  • D. Relational Schema

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

FDB03 · Relations, schemas, instances and metadata

FDB06 · Entities, attributes and ER notation

Correct answers (select all): B, C — B. Database Diagram; C. Schema Diagram

A schema diagram draws relational tables and their key references. Database diagram is also used for that depiction in the course terminology, so both labels qualify. The question explicitly distinguishes a conceptual ER drawing and a textual relational schema from the requested table-reference drawing. This makes the two accepted names compatible without treating every representation of a database as the same artifact.

Evidence in the complete study note

A schema describes names, structures and constraints; an instance, state or snapshot is the data at a particular time. Inserting a student changes the state, while adding a column changes the schema. Metadata is data describing data. The catalog or data dictionary records table definitions, types, constraints and often access privileges and optimizer statistics. A schema diagram draws tables and key references; labels such as database diagram can overlap.FDB03
An entity is a distinguishable real or conceptual thing, such as one student or one course.FDB06
In Chen notation rectangles represent entity types, diamonds relationships and ellipses attributes; double ellipses denote multivalued attributes and dashed ellipses derived attributes.FDB06

D · incorrect — A relational schema defines structures and constraints but need not itself be a diagram.

Question 128

R(A,B,C) has atomic attributes and exactly the dependencies implied by A→B and B→C. A is the sole candidate key; B is not a superkey and C is nonprime. Moving upward through 1NF, 2NF, 3NF and BCNF, which listed normal form is the first that R fails?

  • A. BCNF
  • B. 1NF
  • C. 2NF
  • D. 3NF

Adapted from

  • Database Fundamentals practice questions · Question 64

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Check atomicity, candidate keys and partial dependencies in that order.

Study-guide sections

FDB20 · First and second normal forms

FDB21 · Third normal form, BCNF and higher forms

FDB17 · Functional dependencies and attribute closure

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

FDB20 · First and second normal forms

FDB21 · Third normal form, BCNF and higher forms

FDB17 · Functional dependencies and attribute closure

Correct answer: D — D. 3NF

The atomic relation satisfies 1NF. Its only candidate key is the single attribute A, so no nonprime dependency can involve a proper part of that key, and 2NF holds. However, B→C has a determinant that is not a superkey and a dependent that is nonprime. This violates the stated 3NF test, so 3NF is the listed form it fails.

Evidence in the complete study note

2NF requires 1NF and no nonprime attribute partially dependent on any candidate key. For Enrollment(SID,CID,SName,CTitle,Grade), assume key (SID,CID), SID→SName and CID→CTitle. Split into Student(SID,SName), Course(CID,CTitle) and Enrollment(SID,CID,Grade). Grade still depends on the whole pair. A relation whose every candidate key is single-attribute has no nonprime partial-key dependency. Higher forms inherit 2NF, so no partial dependencies establishes the minimum guaranteed stage only when 1NF is also assumed.FDB20
Full dependency requires all determinant attributes; partial dependency holds through a proper subset.FDB17
With atomic values the highest form is 1NF, even though a transitive dependency also exists.FDB21

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — BCNF also fails, but 3NF is the earlier failed level in the stated hierarchy.

B · incorrect — Atomic values satisfy 1NF.

C · incorrect — A single-attribute candidate key has no nonprime partial-key dependency.

D · correct — B→C violates 3NF because B is not a superkey and C is nonprime.

Question 129

Consider the relation Sale(Date, Customer, Product, Vendor, VendorCity, SalesRep){Date, Customer, Product} is the composite candidate key and the following functional dependencies are also given:Vendor => VendorCity, Product => VendorWhat is the highest normal form of the sale relation?

  • A. 0NF
  • B. 1NF
  • C. 2NF
  • D. 3NF

Source

  • Database Fundamentals practice questions · Question 65

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Test determinants and prime attributes against the stated normal-form rules.

Study-guide sections

FDB21 · Third normal form, BCNF and higher forms

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

FDB21 · Third normal form, BCNF and higher forms

Correct answer: B — 1NF

Assuming atomic values as customary for a relation, Product is a proper subset of the stated composite key and determines non-prime Vendor. Vendor→VendorCity adds another non-key dependency. Product→Vendor alone violates 2NF, so the highest supported form is 1NF. No stated dependency makes these non-prime attributes alternative keys.

Evidence in the complete study note

In Sale(Date,Customer,Product,Vendor,VendorCity,SalesRep), assume key (Date,Customer,Product), Product→Vendor and Vendor→VendorCity. Product is a proper subset of the key, so Vendor and VendorCity violate 2NF. With atomic values the highest form is 1NF, even though a transitive dependency also exists. 4NF addresses nontrivial multivalued dependencies with nonsuperkey determinants; 5NF addresses join dependencies. Do not confuse a multivalued dependency with a list stored inside a cell.FDB21

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — 0NF is not warranted when attributes are atomic. (PDF pp. 15, 17.)

B · correct — 1NF is the highest form under the explicit atomicity assumption. (PDF pp. 15, 17.)

C · incorrect — 2NF fails because Vendor depends on Product alone. (PDF pp. 15, 17.)

D · incorrect — 3NF requires 2NF, which already fails. (PDF pp. 15, 17.)

Question 130

Third normal form is based on the concept of _________

  • A. Closure Dependency
  • B. Transitive Dependency
  • C. Normal Dependency
  • D. Functional Dependency

Source

  • Database Fundamentals practice questions · Question 66

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Test determinants and prime attributes against the stated normal-form rules.

Study-guide sections

FDB21 · Third normal form, BCNF and higher forms

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

FDB21 · Third normal form, BCNF and higher forms

Correct answer: B — Transitive Dependency

The defining extra condition of 3NF over 2NF is non-transitive dependence of non-prime attributes on keys. Functional dependency is the broader framework, but transitive dependency is the particular concept distinguishing this stage.

Evidence in the complete study note

For every nontrivial FD X→A, 3NF permits it when X is a superkey or A is prime. BCNF requires X to be a superkey. The mnemonic whole key and nothing but the key describes the common single-key case; it cannot replace the full tests when keys overlap. Remove dependencies of nonprime facts on nonkey determinants.FDB21
With atomic values the highest form is 1NF, even though a transitive dependency also exists.FDB21
Do not confuse a multivalued dependency with a list stored inside a cell.FDB21

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — Closure dependency is not the stated criterion. (PDF pp. 18.)

B · correct — Transitive dependency is the specific concept addressed. (PDF pp. 18.)

C · incorrect — Normal dependency is not a defined dependency category. (PDF pp. 18.)

D · incorrect — Functional dependency is broader and does not identify the distinctive 3NF condition. (PDF pp. 18.)

Question 131

Which of the following statements does not change the state of the database?

  • A. Where
  • B. Select
  • C. Insert
  • D. Drop

Source

  • Database Fundamentals practice questions · Question 67

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Trace how SELECT forms its result columns and rows.

Study-guide sections

FDB27 · SELECT and its result columns

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

FDB27 · SELECT and its result columns

Correct answer: B — Select

The read-only transaction example uses SELECT to retrieve rows from two tables. SELECT is the complete retrieval statement among these choices; WHERE is only a clause and not a standalone SQL statement. The question is read as asking about valid statements.

Evidence in the complete study note

SELECT Name,Address FROM Customer; produces only those mailing-label fields. SELECT * selects all columns; DISTINCT removes duplicate result rows; AS supplies an alias; ALL permits duplicates and does not mean all columns. SQL normally preserves duplicates without DISTINCT. A query reading table columns needs an appropriate FROM source. A literal-only query such as SELECT 1 may legally omit FROM.FDB27
WHERE alone is a clause rather than a complete read-only statement, so it cannot be compared as an equivalent executable command.FDB27
Typical written clause order is SELECT … FROM … WHERE … GROUP BY … HAVING … ORDER BY ….FDB27

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — WHERE alone is not a complete statement, so it cannot be the requested statement. (PDF pp. 52, 26.)

B · correct — SELECT is demonstrated in a read-only transaction. (PDF pp. 52, 26.)

C · incorrect — INSERT is a manipulation command, not the demonstrated read-only request. (PDF pp. 52, 26.)

D · incorrect — DROP is a structural DDL command, not the demonstrated retrieval. (PDF pp. 52, 26.)

Question 132

A relation is in 1NF and has no nonprime attribute partially dependent on any candidate key. What is the strongest normal form guaranteed by these facts alone?

  • A. First
  • B. BCNF
  • C. Third
  • D. Second

FDB20 · First and second normal forms

FDB21 · Third normal form, BCNF and higher forms

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

FDB20 · First and second normal forms

FDB21 · Third normal form, BCNF and higher forms

Correct answer: D — D. Second

The two stated conditions are exactly the requirements for 2NF: 1NF plus the absence of nonprime partial dependencies on candidate keys. First normal form is also true but is a weaker guarantee. Third normal form and BCNF need additional conditions on other functional dependencies. The relation might satisfy those higher forms, but the given facts alone establish only 2NF.

Evidence in the complete study note

1NF requires atomic values in the chosen relational domains and no repeating groups in cells. It does not mean merely declaring a primary key. A mapped ER design that correctly splits composite and multivalued structures gives ordinary 1NF tables; ER drawing alone guarantees no higher form.FDB20
2NF requires 1NF and no nonprime attribute partially dependent on any candidate key.FDB20
A relation whose every candidate key is single-attribute has no nonprime partial-key dependency.FDB20

A · incorrect — 1NF is given, but 2NF is the stronger guaranteed form.

B · incorrect — BCNF additionally requires all nontrivial determinants to be superkeys.

C · incorrect — 3NF needs further checks on determinants and prime dependents.

D · correct — The conditions directly establish 2NF as the strongest guaranteed form.

Question 133

The minimal set of super key is called

  • A. Foreign key
  • B. None of the choices is correct
  • C. Candidate key
  • D. Primary key

Source

  • Mock exam 1 · Question 18

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Test uniqueness and minimality separately when classifying keys.

Study-guide sections

FDB07 · Superkeys, candidate keys and primary keys

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

FDB07 · Superkeys, candidate keys and primary keys

Correct answer: C — Candidate key

A candidate key is a minimal superkey. Minimal means no removable attribute while retaining uniqueness, not necessarily the fewest attributes among all keys.

Evidence in the complete study note

A superkey uniquely identifies a tuple. A candidate key is a minimal superkey: removing any component loses uniqueness. Minimal means no unnecessary attribute, not necessarily the fewest columns among all keys. A primary key is one selected candidate key; remaining candidates are alternate keys. A composite key contains multiple attributes and need not be the chosen primary key.FDB07

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — Foreign key links relations, not the minimal-superkey definition. (PDF pp. 11.)

B · incorrect — A matching definition is offered. (PDF pp. 11.)

C · correct — Candidate key is the exact definition. (PDF pp. 11.)

D · incorrect — Primary key is the selected candidate key rather than the category of every minimal superkey. (PDF pp. 11.)

Question 134

Assume every foreign-key column is NOT NULL and references a declared primary key. Which statement expresses referential integrity under these assumptions?

  • A. Every primary-key value must match a primary-key value in an associated table
  • B. Every primary-key value must match a foreign-key value in an associated table
  • C. Every foreign-key value must match a primary-key value in an associated table
  • D. Every foreign-key value must match a foreign-key value in an associated table

Adapted from

  • Mock exam 3 · Question 13

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Match the validity requirement to the appropriate constraint definition.

Study-guide sections

FDB15 · Integrity and constraint taxonomy

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

FDB15 · Integrity and constraint taxonomy

Correct answer: C — C. Every foreign-key value must match a primary-key value in an associated table

A foreign key is a reference from a child row to an eligible key in the parent. Because the question makes every reference non-NULL and targets primary keys, each foreign-key value must match a primary-key value. A parent row need not have children, and primary keys do not themselves have to reference another table. Merely matching a foreign-key column is not sufficient.

Evidence in the complete study note

Domain integrity restricts type and allowed values. Key integrity enforces uniqueness. Entity integrity requires every primary-key component to be non-NULL. Referential integrity requires a non-NULL foreign-key value to match an eligible referenced unique/candidate key; self-references are allowed. A parent need not have a child.FDB15

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — A primary key can exist without referencing another table.

B · incorrect — A parent can have no child references.

C · correct — Every non-NULL child reference must match its targeted parent primary key.

D · incorrect — The target must be an eligible referenced key, not an arbitrary foreign-key column.

Question 135

Which of the following is generally used for performing tasks like creating the structure of the relations, deleting relation?

  • A. DML(Data Manipulation Language)
  • B. Query
  • C. Relational Schema
  • D. DDL(Data Definition Language)

Study-guide sections

FDB26 · SQL command categories and exact scope

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

FDB26 · SQL command categories and exact scope

Correct answer: D — DDL(Data Definition Language)

Creating/deleting relation structure falls under DDL's structural command category. It is distinguished from data manipulation and from the schema description itself.

Evidence in the complete study note

DDL changes structure: CREATE, ALTER, DROP, commonly RENAME and TRUNCATE in textbook classifications. DML retrieves or changes data: SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE. Some texts separate SELECT as DQL. DCL manages privileges through GRANT and REVOKE. TCL includes COMMIT, ROLLBACK and SAVEPOINT. Classify a statement by its effect, not its length.FDB26

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — DML is the data-operation category. (PDF pp. 26.)

B · incorrect — Query is not the schema-definition category. (PDF pp. 26.)

C · incorrect — A relational schema is a description, not a command language. (PDF pp. 26.)

D · correct — DDL is the named structural-language category. (PDF pp. 26.)

Question 138

In ER diagram Diamond/Ellipses represent

  • A. Relationship sets/Attributes
  • B. Entity sets/Attributes
  • C. Attributes/Relationships sets
  • D. Relationship sets/Entity sets

Source

  • Database Fundamentals practice questions · Question 63

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Review entity, attribute and relationship notation before interpreting the model.

Study-guide sections

FDB06 · Entities, attributes and ER notation

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

FDB06 · Entities, attributes and ER notation

Correct answer: A — Diamonds represent relationship sets; ellipses represent attributes.

FDB06 maps rectangles to entity types, diamonds to relationships and ellipses to attributes in Chen notation. The question asks for the diamond/ellipse pair in that order. A preserves that order and meaning; the other choices either substitute entity sets or reverse the symbol meanings. A diagram-specific legend would override the generic convention.

Evidence in the complete study note

An entity is a distinguishable real or conceptual thing, such as one student or one course. An entity type describes common properties; its current collection of entities is an entity set. A relationship associates entities. In Chen notation rectangles represent entity types, diamonds relationships and ellipses attributes; double ellipses denote multivalued attributes and dashed ellipses derived attributes. Always inspect the diagram legend.FDB06

Choice by choice

A · correct — The two shapes map to relationships and attributes respectively in Chen notation.

B · incorrect — Entity types use rectangles, so a diamond does not represent the first item listed.

C · incorrect — This reverses the requested meanings: diamonds indicate relationships, while ellipses indicate attributes.

D · incorrect — The first meaning fits, but ellipses represent attributes rather than entity sets.

Question 139

A relation is in 2NF if:

  • A. All the values of non-key attributes are dependent fully on the candidate key.
  • B. Any non-key attribute that are dependent on only part of the candidate key should be moved to another relation where the partial key is the actual full key.

FDB20 · First and second normal forms

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

FDB20 · First and second normal forms

Correct answer: D — All of the above.

Second normal form requires 1NF and full dependence of each non-prime attribute on candidate keys; partial dependencies must be moved to another relation. A states the criterion, B the repair, and C the prerequisite; D collects all three. A alone is not a complete definition without C.

Evidence in the complete study note

2NF requires 1NF and no nonprime attribute partially dependent on any candidate key. For Enrollment(SID,CID,SName,CTitle,Grade), assume key (SID,CID), SID→SName and CID→CTitle. Split into Student(SID,SName), Course(CID,CTitle) and Enrollment(SID,CID,Grade). Grade still depends on the whole pair. A relation whose every candidate key is single-attribute has no nonprime partial-key dependency. Higher forms inherit 2NF, so no partial dependencies establishes the minimum guaranteed stage only when 1NF is also assumed.FDB20

C · incorrect — 1NF is expressly required. (PDF pp. 17.)

D · correct — All three agree with the module's definition and repair rule. (PDF pp. 17.)

Question 140

Which one of the following is NOT a single level ordered index?

  • A. Secondary index
  • B. Primary index
  • C. Clustering index
  • D. Multilevel index

Sources

  • MOE Computer Science Exit Exam · Question 5-1

  • National Computer Science Exit Exam · Question 32

  • National Computer Science Exit Exam

  • Computer Science Exit Exam practice collection · Question 42

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Compare index organization with its access benefits and maintenance costs.

Study-guide sections

FDB25 · Ordered, dense, sparse and multilevel indexes

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

FDB25 · Ordered, dense, sparse and multilevel indexes

Correct answer: D — Multilevel index

In this single-level ordered-index classification, the listed types are primary, clustering and secondary indexes. A multilevel index is the exception to that listed single-level classification.

Evidence in the complete study note

In ordered-file indexing terminology, a primary ordered index is on the file’s ordering key and can have one entry per page, hence sparse. A clustering index is on a nonunique ordering field, often with an entry per distinct value. Secondary indexes provide other access paths and must represent all searchable values with record pointers or pointer lists. A dense index represents each search value; sparse indexes rely on ordered data between entries. Multilevel indexes index lower index levels. B/B+ trees support ordered and range search; hashing targets equality; bitmap indexes suit some analytic workloads. No one type is universally best.FDB25

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — Secondary is listed as single level. (PDF pp. 21.)

B · incorrect — Primary is listed as single level. (PDF pp. 21.)

C · incorrect — Clustering is listed as single level. (PDF pp. 21.)

D · correct — Multilevel is outside the specified single-level group. (PDF pp. 21.)

Question 143

What does program data independence in the database approach entail?

  • A. The application may be affected when data changes
  • B. Data and applications are defined separately
  • C. Programs can create redundant data independently
  • D. Both program and data are defined together

Sources

  • MOE Computer Science Exit Exam · Question 14-3

  • National Computer Science Exit Exam · Question 50

  • National Computer Science Exit Exam

  • MOE Computer Science Exit Exam

  • Computer Science Exit Exam practice collection · Question 45

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Trace which schema levels a proposed change affects.

Study-guide sections

FDB04 · Three-schema architecture and data independence

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

FDB04 · Three-schema architecture and data independence

Correct answer: B — Data and applications are defined separately

Program/data insulation separates application definitions from data definitions, allowing schema changes without corresponding higher-level changes under the relevant independence conditions.

Evidence in the complete study note

Physical data independence permits changing internal storage without changing the conceptual schema, for example adding an index. Logical data independence permits conceptual changes while preserving affected external views and application interfaces, for example splitting a table while maintaining a compatible view. It is harder because applications depend on logical meaning. Data abstraction hides irrelevant details; program–data insulation separates data definitions from application code.FDB04

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — Says changes necessarily affect applications, contrary to insulation. (PDF pp. 5, 6, 8.)

B · correct — Separate definitions express program/data insulation. (PDF pp. 5, 6, 8.)

C · incorrect — Creating redundant data independently is not the defined independence. (PDF pp. 5, 6, 8.)

D · incorrect — Defining both together contradicts insulation. (PDF pp. 5, 6, 8.)

Question 144

Which statements are NOT correct descriptions of a fan trap or its general remedy in an ER diagram? Select all that apply. Treat B as a warning pattern, not a sufficient condition.

  • A. It occurs when there is no pathway between related entities
  • B. Highly likely when more than two one-to-many relationships fan out from an entity
  • C. When there is ambiguous pathway between some entity occurrences in related entity types
  • D. It can be resolved by readjusting the model so that no one-to-many relationships fan out from an entity

Adapted from

  • MOE Computer Science Exit Exam · Question 20-3

  • National Computer Science Exit Exam · Question 24

  • National Computer Science Exit Exam

  • MOE Computer Science Exit Exam

  • Computer Science Exit Exam practice collection · Question 46

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Trace the actual relationship paths and check whether the requested association follows.

Study-guide sections

FDB14 · Fan traps and chasm traps

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

FDB14 · Fan traps and chasm traps

Correct answers (select all): A, D — A. It occurs when there is no pathway between related entities; D. It can be resolved by readjusting the model so that no one-to-many relationships fan out from an entity

A fan trap concerns an ambiguous association inferred through branches from a common entity. A missing or broken path is instead a chasm-type issue. Also, legitimate one-to-many branches need not all be eliminated: the model should record the actual missing association. Multiple branches can warn of a fan trap, and ambiguity is its defining issue, so B and C are not false.

Evidence in the complete study note

A fan trap occurs when two 1:N relationships fan from a common entity and a path suggests an association it cannot determine. Department→Employee and Department→Project do not reveal which employee works on which project; joining through Department pairs every employee with every project in that department. Record WorksOn explicitly when that is the fact needed.FDB14
A missing path describes a chasm, not the defining ambiguity of a fan.FDB14
Multiple 1:N relationships are legitimate; eliminating all fans is not a universal repair.FDB14

Choice by choice

A · correct — This is false about fan traps: a missing or broken path describes a chasm-type problem.

B · incorrect — Several 1:N branches from a common entity can create the ambiguous association characteristic of fan traps.

C · incorrect — Ambiguity about associations between entity occurrences is the defining fan-trap problem.

D · correct — This is false as a general remedy: valid 1:N fans may remain after explicitly modeling the required association.

Question 145

In which situation does using a DBMS become costly and not recommended?

  • B. In situation where more than one user should access the database
  • C. To deny access to unauthorized users
  • D. To control redundancy

Study-guide sections

FDB01 · Database approach, benefits and costs

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

FDB01 · Database approach, benefits and costs

Correct answer: A — Severely storage-constrained embedded systems can make a DBMS too costly.

FDB01 says storage, administration and complexity may outweigh DBMS benefits in a tiny fixed application, while also recognizing lightweight embedded DBMSs. A is therefore the listed situation where resource constraints can make a DBMS inappropriate. The other scenarios are central reasons for using a DBMS: sharing, authorization and redundancy control. Embedded use alone does not forbid a DBMS.

Evidence in the complete study note

A database is a coherent collection of related facts with meaning for an application. A DBMS is software that defines, stores, retrieves, updates, protects and shares that data. The database is the content; the DBMS manages it. A database system also includes applications, people and infrastructure.FDB01
Complexity, administration, storage and licensing can outweigh benefits for a tiny fixed application; lightweight embedded DBMSs also exist.FDB01
It controls redundancy rather than guaranteeing zero duplication.FDB01

Choice by choice

A · correct — Severely limited storage can make DBMS overhead unjustified, though lightweight embedded DBMSs may still fit.

B · incorrect — Controlled multiuser access is a benefit supplied by database management rather than a reason to avoid it.

C · incorrect — Authorization helps deny unauthorized access and is a reason to use DBMS protection.

D · incorrect — Controlling redundancy is a benefit of shared database management rather than a contraindication.

Question 146

Indexes in database tables speed up the _______ operations.

  • A. Read operations
  • B. Delete operations
  • C. Update operations
  • D. Write operations

  • Computer Science Exit Exam · Sene 2016 · Question 3-1

  • Computer Science Exit Exam practice collection · Question 56

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Compare index organization with its access benefits and maintenance costs.

Study-guide sections

FDB25 · Ordered, dense, sparse and multilevel indexes

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

FDB25 · Ordered, dense, sparse and multilevel indexes

Correct answer: A — Read operations

Indexes are auxiliary access structures explicitly used to speed record retrieval for search conditions. This is a read-performance purpose; it does not claim that maintaining indexes makes every change operation faster.

Evidence in the complete study note

An index stores search keys with pointers or row identifiers. It often speeds selective retrieval but consumes space and must be maintained on writes. A full scan can win for a tiny table or a query returning most rows. Indexes do not themselves establish referential integrity.FDB25
Secondary indexes provide other access paths and must represent all searchable values with record pointers or pointer lists.FDB25
A dense index represents each search value; sparse indexes rely on ordered data between entries.FDB25

Choice by choice

A · correct — Read retrieval is the stated purpose. (PDF pp. 21.)

B · incorrect — Deletion may require maintenance; it is not the stated general benefit. (PDF pp. 21.)

C · incorrect — Updating data is not the stated general purpose. (PDF pp. 21.)

D · incorrect — Writing is not the stated retrieval purpose. (PDF pp. 21.)

Question 147

An e-commerce database already has a suitable normalized schema. Customer-order queries repeatedly select a small fraction of rows by a search key. Which technique directly adds a maintained search path for those lookups?

  • A. Normalization
  • B. Denormalization
  • C. Partitioning
  • D. Indexing

Adapted from

  • Computer Science Exit Exam · Sene 2016 · Question 9-1

  • Computer Science Exit Exam practice collection · Question 68

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Compare design techniques by the specific problem each solves.

Study-guide sections

FDB38 · Choose a design technique from the requirement

FDB25 · Ordered, dense, sparse and multilevel indexes

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

FDB38 · Choose a design technique from the requirement

FDB25 · Ordered, dense, sparse and multilevel indexes

Correct answer: D — D. Indexing

Indexing stores search keys and pointers that support selective retrieval without scanning every row. That directly meets the requested search-path improvement. Normalization addresses dependency anomalies, denormalization stores redundant or precomputed facts, and partitioning divides a dataset. Those techniques can be useful elsewhere, but the specified task is to add an access path to the existing normalized schema.

Evidence in the complete study note

Normalization reduces dependency-driven anomalies. Indexing supplies search paths. Denormalization deliberately repeats or precomputes facts to reduce selected read costs, increasing maintenance work. Partitioning divides rows or columns; replication creates copies to improve locality or availability while complicating updates. These techniques can coexist. An e-commerce or gaming label alone does not select one.FDB38
An index stores search keys with pointers or row identifiers.FDB25
It often speeds selective retrieval but consumes space and must be maintained on writes.FDB25

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — Normalization organizes dependencies; it does not add the requested search path.

B · incorrect — Denormalization repeats or precomputes facts rather than directly adding an index path.

C · incorrect — Partitioning divides the dataset rather than adding the requested maintained search-key structure.

D · correct — Indexing directly supplies the selective lookup search path.

Question 148

Which SQL command is used to modify existing data in a table?

  • A. ALTER
  • B. UPDATE
  • C. MODIFY
  • D. CHANGE

Sources

  • Computer Science Exit Exam · Sene 2016 · Question 16-4

  • Computer Science Exit Exam practice collection · Question 54

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Track which rows each data-changing command affects.

Study-guide sections

FDB29 · INSERT, UPDATE and DELETE

FDB26 · SQL command categories and exact scope

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

FDB29 · INSERT, UPDATE and DELETE

FDB26 · SQL command categories and exact scope

Correct answer: B — UPDATE

UPDATE changes existing row values using a target table, SET assignments and an optional WHERE predicate. ALTER instead changes an object's definition. The bare words MODIFY and CHANGE do not form the UPDATE statement required for changing stored row values in the stated SQL forms. They should not be treated as synonyms for the command merely because they describe the task in everyday language. B names the actual row-update command.

Evidence in the complete study note

UPDATE Book SET Price = 600 WHERE ID = 'B001'; changes matching rows. UPDATE without WHERE targets every row.FDB29
DDL changes structure: CREATE, ALTER, DROP, commonly RENAME and TRUNCATE in textbook classifications. DML retrieves or changes data: SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE.FDB26

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — Incorrect: ALTER changes schema definitions rather than stored row values.

B · correct — Correct: UPDATE changes existing rows using SET and, when needed, WHERE.

C · incorrect — Incorrect: MODIFY is not the UPDATE command required for the stated row-value operation.

D · incorrect — Incorrect: CHANGE is not the UPDATE command required for the stated row-value operation.

Question 149

Which is the first normal form in the usual normalization sequence that requires atomic values and no repeating groups?

  • A. Second Normal Form (2NF)
  • B. Third Normal Form (3NF)
  • C. Boyce–Codd Normal Form (BCNF)
  • D. First Normal Form (1NF)

Adapted from

  • Computer Science Exit Exam · Sene 2016 · Question 17-1

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Check atomicity, candidate keys and partial dependencies in that order.

Study-guide sections

FDB20 · First and second normal forms

FDB21 · Third normal form, BCNF and higher forms

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

FDB20 · First and second normal forms

FDB21 · Third normal form, BCNF and higher forms

Correct answer: D — First Normal Form (1NF)

First normal form is the first stage requiring atomic values and no repeating groups in cells. It does not by itself remove partial or transitive functional dependencies. Second normal form adds the full-dependency condition, third normal form adds the determinant/prime-attribute rule, and BCNF requires every nontrivial determinant to be a superkey. These stronger forms inherit atomicity, but the question asks for the first stage defining it.

Evidence in the complete study note

1NF requires atomic values in the chosen relational domains and no repeating groups in cells.FDB20
2NF requires 1NF and no nonprime attribute partially dependent on any candidate key.FDB20
For every nontrivial FD X→A, 3NF permits it when X is a superkey or A is prime. BCNF requires X to be a superkey. The mnemonic whole key and nothing but the key describes the common single-key case; it cannot replace the full tests when keys overlap. Remove dependencies of nonprime facts on nonkey determinants.FDB21

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — Incorrect: 2NF inherits atomicity and additionally excludes nonprime partial dependencies; it is a later stage.

B · incorrect — Incorrect: 3NF inherits the earlier atomicity condition and adds a stronger dependency test.

C · incorrect — Incorrect: BCNF adds the superkey-determinant rule and is not the first stage requiring atomicity.

D · correct — Correct: 1NF is the first stage defining atomic values and no repeating groups.

Question 150

What is the purpose of normalization in database design?

  • A. To increase data redundancy
  • B. To decrease data integrity
  • C. To eliminate data duplication
  • D. To simplify data retrieval

Sources

  • Computer Science Exit Exam · Sene 2016 · Question 17-3

  • Computer Science MCQ practice collection · Question 131

  • Computer Science MCQ practice collection · Question 152

  • Computer Science Exit Exam practice collection · Question 53

Alternative version 1

  • A. To reduce redundancy and improve data integrity
  • B. To speed up query performance
  • C. To create complex relationships between tables
  • D. To encrypt sensitive data
Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Distinguish dependency-based design problems from the contents of one sample table.

Study-guide sections

FDB19 · Insertion, deletion and update anomalies

FDB38 · Choose a design technique from the requirement

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

FDB19 · Insertion, deletion and update anomalies

FDB38 · Choose a design technique from the requirement

Correct answer: C — To eliminate data duplication

Normalization systematically decomposes relations to eliminate or reduce duplication and insertion/update/deletion anomalies. Eliminate is understood as the design goal, not a claim that every possible repeated value or necessary key reference disappears.

Evidence in the complete study note

Suppose Enrollment repeats StudentName for every course. An update anomaly requires changing every copy of the student's name; missed copies disagree. An insertion anomaly prevents recording a new student until some enrollment exists. A deletion anomaly loses the only record of a student's details when their last enrollment is deleted. Revoke anomaly is not a normalization anomaly.FDB19
Adding a generated row ID may make insertion technically possible, but does not remove repeated facts or fix the dependency design.FDB19
Reduced redundancy can improve integrity and storage use, but extra keys and joins mean neither storage savings nor faster retrieval is guaranteed.FDB19

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — Increasing redundancy reverses the goal. (PDF pp. 16.)

B · incorrect — Decreasing integrity reverses the anomaly-prevention goal. (PDF pp. 16.)

C · correct — Eliminating duplicated data is directly stated. (PDF pp. 16.)

D · incorrect — Simplifying retrieval is not the stated normalization purpose and does not follow automatically from decomposition. (PDF pp. 16.)

Alternative version 1

Correct answer: A — To reduce redundancy and improve data integrity

Normalization systematically decomposes relations to eliminate or reduce duplication and insertion/update/deletion anomalies. Eliminate is understood as the design goal, not a claim that every possible repeated value or necessary key reference disappears.

Suppose Enrollment repeats StudentName for every course. An update anomaly requires changing every copy of the student's name; missed copies disagree. An insertion anomaly prevents recording a new student until some enrollment exists. A deletion anomaly loses the only record of a student's details when their last enrollment is deleted. Revoke anomaly is not a normalization anomaly.FDB19

Reduced redundancy can improve integrity and storage use, but extra keys and joins mean neither storage savings nor faster retrieval is guaranteed.FDB19

Adding a generated row ID may make insertion technically possible, but does not remove repeated facts or fix the dependency design.FDB19

A · correct — Reducing redundancy and preventing anomalies is the normalization goal (PDF p.16).

B · incorrect — Query performance is not the stated normalization purpose; decomposition can require additional joins.

C · incorrect — Relationships support decomposition but creating complexity is not its goal.

D · incorrect — Encryption is a security mechanism, not normalization.

FDB06 · Entities, attributes and ER notation

Correct answer: C — Entities

The ER model uses entities, attributes and relationships; the actual physical/conceptual objects are entities. Objects is a generic word, while entities is the model-specific category requested.

Evidence in the complete study note

An entity is a distinguishable real or conceptual thing, such as one student or one course. An entity type describes common properties; its current collection of entities is an entity set. Question 153

What is the primary key in a relational database?

  • A. A unique identifier for each row in a table
  • B. A foreign key from another table
  • C. A key used for encryption
  • D. An index on a non-unique column

Sources

  • Computer Science Exit Exam · Sene 2016 · Question 24-3

  • Computer Science Exit Exam practice collection · Question 51

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Test uniqueness and minimality separately when classifying keys.

Study-guide sections

FDB07 · Superkeys, candidate keys and primary keys

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

FDB07 · Superkeys, candidate keys and primary keys

Correct answer: A — A unique identifier for each row in a table

A primary key is the selected candidate key used to identify tuples uniquely; its values must be unique and non-null. The key may consist of more than one attribute.

Evidence in the complete study note

A superkey uniquely identifies a tuple. A candidate key is a minimal superkey: removing any component loses uniqueness. Minimal means no unnecessary attribute, not necessarily the fewest columns among all keys. A primary key is one selected candidate key; remaining candidates are alternate keys. A composite key contains multiple attributes and need not be the chosen primary key.FDB07
A table can have several candidates and foreign keys but at most one declared primary-key constraint, possibly containing several columns.FDB07
If ID is unique, both {ID} and {ID,City} are superkeys, but only {ID} is minimal.FDB07

Choice by choice

A · correct — Uniquely identifies each row, consistent with the key definition. (PDF pp. 11, 14.)

B · incorrect — A foreign key serves a reference role and need not be this table's primary key. (PDF pp. 11, 14.)

C · incorrect — Encryption keys have a different security role. (PDF pp. 11, 14.)

D · incorrect — A non-unique index does not meet key uniqueness. (PDF pp. 11, 14.)

Question 156

A relation has atomic values with no repeating groups, and no nonprime attribute partially depends on any candidate key. What is the strongest normal form guaranteed by these facts alone?

  • A. 4NF
  • B. 2NF
  • C. 1NF
  • D. 3NF

Adapted from

  • Computer Science model exam collection · Question 40

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Check atomicity, candidate keys and partial dependencies in that order.

Study-guide sections

FDB20 · First and second normal forms

FDB21 · Third normal form, BCNF and higher forms

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

FDB20 · First and second normal forms

FDB21 · Third normal form, BCNF and higher forms

Correct answer: B — B. 2NF

Atomic values without repeating groups establish 1NF. Adding the absence of nonprime partial dependencies establishes 2NF. Higher normal forms have additional requirements involving nonkey determinants or multivalued dependencies, and none of those requirements follows from the stated facts. Thus 2NF is the strongest guaranteed level, even though the relation could also happen to satisfy higher forms.

Evidence in the complete study note

1NF requires atomic values in the chosen relational domains and no repeating groups in cells. It does not mean merely declaring a primary key. A mapped ER design that correctly splits composite and multivalued structures gives ordinary 1NF tables; ER drawing alone guarantees no higher form.FDB20
2NF requires 1NF and no nonprime attribute partially dependent on any candidate key.FDB20
Higher forms inherit 2NF, so no partial dependencies establishes the minimum guaranteed stage only when 1NF is also assumed.FDB20

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — No restriction on nontrivial multivalued dependencies has been established.

B · correct — 1NF plus the absence of nonprime partial dependencies guarantees 2NF.

C · incorrect — 1NF holds, but 2NF is the stronger guarantee.

D · incorrect — 3NF requires additional determinant/prime-attribute conditions.

FDB03 · Relations, schemas, instances and metadata

FDB04 · Three-schema architecture and data independence

Correct answer: B — @-tTM Application-data dependency

Database systems support sharing, self-description and abstraction/insulation. Application-data dependency is the opposite of insulation and independence.

Evidence in the complete study note

A database is a coherent collection of related facts with meaning for an application. A DBMS is software that defines, stores, retrieves, updates, protects and shares that data. Data abstraction hides irrelevant details; program–data insulation separates data definitions from application code.FDB04

Question 159

For HOTEL(Hotel_id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY, Hotel_Name STRING, Sub_city STRING), which statement correctly inserts one row? Assume Hotel_id = 1 is unused and no other constraints prevent the insert.

  • A. INSERT INTO HOTEL(Hotel_id,Hotel_Name,Sub_city) Values('Hilton', Yeka')
  • B. INSERT INTO HOTEL(Hotel_id,Hotel_Name,Sub_city) Values (1,'Hilton','Yeka')
  • C. INSERT INTO HOTEL(1,'Hilton','Yeka')
  • D. INSERT Values INTO HOTEL(Hotel_id,Hotel_Name,Sub_city) Values(1,'Hilton','Yeka')

Adapted from

  • Computer Science model exam collection · Question 64

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Track which rows each data-changing command affects.

Study-guide sections

FDB29 · INSERT, UPDATE and DELETE

FDB07 · Superkeys, candidate keys and primary keys

FDB15 · Integrity and constraint taxonomy

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

FDB29 · INSERT, UPDATE and DELETE

FDB07 · Superkeys, candidate keys and primary keys

FDB15 · Integrity and constraint taxonomy

Correct answer: B — INSERT INTO HOTEL(Hotel_id,Hotel_Name,Sub_city) Values (1,'Hilton','Yeka')

An INSERT must match each named column with a compatible value and follow INSERT INTO ... VALUES syntax. B supplies the integer identifier and the two text values in the named order; the stated unused-key assumption removes a possible primary-key conflict. A supplies too few values and has damaged string quoting. C puts values where column names belong and omits VALUES, while D places an extra Values token before INTO. These are incorrect statements, not unresolved alternatives.

Evidence in the complete study note

INSERT INTO Hotel(HotelID,HotelName,SubCity) VALUES (1,'Hilton','Yeka'); pairs each named column with a compatible value. Counts and types must match; keys and other constraints still apply. Without a column list, values must follow the table's declared column order.FDB29
Domain integrity restricts type and allowed values. Key integrity enforces uniqueness. Entity integrity requires every primary-key component to be non-NULL. Referential integrity requires a non-NULL foreign-key value to match an eligible referenced unique/candidate key; self-references are allowed. A parent need not have a child.FDB15

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — Incorrect: three named columns require three compatible values; this text supplies only two and also has malformed quoting.

B · correct — Correct: it supplies an unused integer key and two strings in the named column order with the required INSERT syntax.

C · incorrect — Incorrect: literal values occupy the column-list position and the VALUES clause is absent.

D · incorrect — Incorrect: INSERT Values INTO has an extra token before INTO and breaks the required INSERT structure.

Question 160

DORM(DormID, FloorNumber) has primary key DormID. STUDENT(IdNo, Name, Department, DormID) has primary key IdNo. Which table-constraint fragment correctly declares STUDENT.DormID as a reference to DORM? Capacity six is enforced separately; the surrounding CREATE TABLE syntax is supplied.

  • A. FOREIGN KEY (DormID) REFERENCED BY DORM(DormID)
  • B. DORM table is a referencing relation
  • C. FOREIGN KEY (DormID) REFERENCES DORM(DormID)
  • D. STUDENT table is a referenced relation

Adapted from

  • Computer Science model exam collection · Question 79

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Trace keys and cardinalities through the integrated schema.

Study-guide sections

FDB39 · Integrated design: student registration and dormitory allocation

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

FDB39 · Integrated design: student registration and dormitory allocation

Correct answer: C — C. FOREIGN KEY (DormID) REFERENCES DORM(DormID)

The student row stores the dorm identifier, making STUDENT the referencing child and DORM the referenced parent. The required clause names the child column and uses REFERENCES to identify Dorm's key. C has this syntax after the naming repair. The foreign key validates a dorm reference; it does not by itself enforce the six-student capacity rule, which the question handles separately.

Evidence in the complete study note

Use Department(DeptID,Name), Student(SID,Name,DeptID,DormID), Course(CID,Title), Enrollment(SID,CID,Grade) and Dorm(DormID,FloorNumber). Declare all entity IDs primary keys; Student.DeptID and Student.DormID reference their parents; Enrollment has composite key (SID,CID) and both references. Make DormID nullable until assignment. Separate StudentPhone(SID,Phone) for multiple contacts. Normalize repeated department/course facts into their owning tables. A simple foreign key does not enforce the six-student capacity; use a concurrency-safe trigger, allocation transaction or explicit slot design with six unique slots per dorm.FDB39
STUDENT is the referencing child; DORM is referenced.FDB39
Consistent names and an added Student.DormID column are required before execution.FDB39

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — REFERENCED BY is not the required child-to-parent clause.

B · incorrect — DORM is the referenced parent, not the referencing child.

C · correct — REFERENCES DORM(DormID) correctly targets the parent key.

D · incorrect — STUDENT is the referencing child.

Question 161

Which one of the following is an appropriate sequence of database design processes?

  • A. Logical database design, Enterprise data modeling, Physical database design, Database implementation
  • B. Enterprise data modeling, Logical database design, Database implementation, Physical database design
  • C. Logical database design, Enterprise data modeling, Physical database design, Database implementation
  • D. Enterprise data modeling, Logical database design, Physical database design, Database implementation

Source

  • Computer Science model exam collection · Question 96

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Identify the design phase from its inputs and intended deliverable.

Study-guide sections

FDB05 · Requirements through implementation

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

FDB05 · Requirements through implementation

Correct answer: D — Enterprise modeling → logical design → physical design → implementation.

FDB05 starts with business requirements and conceptual ER modeling, then maps to relations and constraints in logical design, chooses storage and indexes in physical design, and creates/loads actual objects in implementation. D preserves that order. The other choices either begin logical design before enterprise modeling or place implementation before the storage decisions it must implement.

Evidence in the complete study note

First collect business facts, required transactions, reports, volumes, privacy rules and quality targets. Conceptual design turns them into an ER/EER model. Logical design maps the conceptual model to relations, keys and constraints and checks normalization. Physical design chooses file organization, indexes and storage. Implementation creates and loads the actual objects; testing checks requirements.FDB05

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — Logical design should use the conceptual enterprise model rather than precede its creation.

B · incorrect — Physical storage decisions precede implementation, not follow it in the stated design sequence.

C · incorrect — This duplicates A and again places logical design before enterprise modeling.

D · correct — It follows conceptual enterprise modeling, relational mapping, storage design and actual implementation.

Question 162

Which of the following is a primary key in a relational database?

  • A. A unique identifier for a row in a table
  • B. A foreign key from another table
  • C. A non-unique attribute in a table
  • D. A combination of attributes in a table

Source

  • Computer Science MCQ practice collection · Question 132

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Test uniqueness and minimality separately when classifying keys.

Study-guide sections

FDB07 · Superkeys, candidate keys and primary keys

FDB16 · Write and enforce SQL constraints

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

FDB07 · Superkeys, candidate keys and primary keys

FDB16 · Write and enforce SQL constraints

Correct answer: A — A unique identifier for a row in a table

A primary key is selected from candidate keys to uniquely identify a row and must be unique/non-null. Multiple attributes alone do not make a set a primary key unless those requirements and selection hold.

Evidence in the complete study note

A superkey uniquely identifies a tuple. A candidate key is a minimal superkey: removing any component loses uniqueness. Minimal means no unnecessary attribute, not necessarily the fewest columns among all keys. A primary key is one selected candidate key; remaining candidates are alternate keys. A composite key contains multiple attributes and need not be the chosen primary key.FDB07
A table can have several candidates and foreign keys but at most one declared primary-key constraint, possibly containing several columns.FDB07
PRIMARY KEY implies uniqueness and non-NULL.FDB16

Choice by choice

A · correct — Gives the essential identifying property. (PDF pp. 11, 14.)

B · incorrect — A foreign key is a reference and is not necessarily the selected primary identifier. (PDF pp. 11, 14.)

C · incorrect — Non-unique attributes cannot uniquely identify rows. (PDF pp. 11, 14.)

D · incorrect — A combination can be a composite primary key, but an arbitrary combination is not sufficient. (PDF pp. 11, 14.)

Question 164

In the usual normalization sequence, which is the first normal form requiring every nonprime attribute to depend fully on every candidate key, rather than on a proper subset?

  • A. First normal form (1NF)
  • B. Second normal form (2NF)
  • C. Third normal form (3NF)
  • D. Boyce-Codd normal form (BCNF)

Adapted from

  • Computer Science MCQ practice collection · Question 134

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Check atomicity, candidate keys and partial dependencies in that order.

Study-guide sections

FDB20 · First and second normal forms

FDB21 · Third normal form, BCNF and higher forms

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

FDB20 · First and second normal forms

FDB21 · Third normal form, BCNF and higher forms

Correct answer: B — Second normal form (2NF)

Second normal form is the first stage requiring atomic relations to have no nonprime attribute partially dependent on any candidate key. First normal form addresses atomic values, so it does not impose that dependency rule. Third normal form and BCNF are stronger stages that inherit the absence of partial dependencies, but they are not the first stage introducing it. BCNF specifically requires every nontrivial functional-dependency determinant to be a superkey.

Evidence in the complete study note

1NF requires atomic values in the chosen relational domains and no repeating groups in cells.FDB20
2NF requires 1NF and no nonprime attribute partially dependent on any candidate key.FDB20
For every nontrivial FD X→A, 3NF permits it when X is a superkey or A is prime. BCNF requires X to be a superkey. The mnemonic whole key and nothing but the key describes the common single-key case; it cannot replace the full tests when keys overlap. Remove dependencies of nonprime facts on nonkey determinants.FDB21

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — Incorrect: 1NF requires atomic values but does not exclude partial dependencies.

B · correct — Correct: 2NF is the first stage excluding nonprime partial dependencies on candidate keys.

C · incorrect — Incorrect: 3NF inherits 2NF and imposes its additional determinant/prime-attribute condition; it is not the first stage.

D · incorrect — Incorrect: BCNF requires every nontrivial determinant to be a superkey; it is stronger than the first stage imposing the partial-dependency rule.

Question 165

What defines a composite key in database design?

  • A. A key made up of two or more attributes
  • B. A foreign key that references multiple tables
  • C. A unique identifier for a column in a table
  • D. A key used for data encryption

FDB07 · Superkeys, candidate keys and primary keys

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

FDB07 · Superkeys, candidate keys and primary keys

Correct answer: A — A. A key made up of two or more attributes

Composite describes the number of attributes in a key: it contains two or more attributes. It does not mean the key has necessarily been selected as the primary key, nor does it refer to the number of tables reached by a reference. A column name alone is not a multicolumn identification key. Encryption uses a different meaning of key.

Evidence in the complete study note

A superkey uniquely identifies a tuple. A candidate key is a minimal superkey: removing any component loses uniqueness. Minimal means no unnecessary attribute, not necessarily the fewest columns among all keys. A primary key is one selected candidate key; remaining candidates are alternate keys. A composite key contains multiple attributes and need not be the chosen primary key.FDB07
A composite-key example must include multiple columns; a single-column key is not composite.FDB07
A table can have several candidates and foreign keys but at most one declared primary-key constraint, possibly containing several columns.FDB07

Choice by choice

A · correct — Two or more key attributes are the defining feature.

B · incorrect — The number of referenced tables does not define composition.

C · incorrect — A column identifier does not describe a multicolumn key.

D · incorrect — An encryption key is a different concept.

Question 167

What is a data dictionary in database design?

  • A. A database table that stores user information
  • B. A collection of tables in a database
  • C. A file system used to store database files
  • D. A repository of metadata about the database structure

Source

  • Computer Science MCQ practice collection · Question 137

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Distinguish a relation's structure from its current contents and its descriptive metadata.

Study-guide sections

FDB03 · Relations, schemas, instances and metadata

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

FDB03 · Relations, schemas, instances and metadata

Correct answer: D — A repository of database metadata.

A data dictionary describes the database rather than serving as ordinary application data storage. FDB03 says it records definitions, types, constraints and often privileges and optimizer statistics. User information may appear in an application table, but that is not the dictionary definition. Nor is a dictionary simply all tables or the physical file system.

Evidence in the complete study note

A schema describes names, structures and constraints; an instance, state or snapshot is the data at a particular time. Inserting a student changes the state, while adding a column changes the schema. Metadata is data describing data. The catalog or data dictionary records table definitions, types, constraints and often access privileges and optimizer statistics. A schema diagram draws tables and key references; labels such as database diagram can overlap.FDB03

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — Ordinary user records are application data, not the defining purpose of a metadata repository.

B · incorrect — A collection of tables is not specifically the repository describing those tables and constraints.

C · incorrect — The physical file system stores files; a dictionary records descriptive database metadata.

D · correct — The dictionary is the repository describing database structure, data types and constraints.

Question 168

Which of the following is an example of a database constraint?

  • A. UNIQUE
  • B. SELECT
  • C. INSERT
  • D. DELETE

Source

  • Computer Science MCQ practice collection · Question 138

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Read each SQL constraint's scope and enforcement rule.

Study-guide sections

FDB16 · Write and enforce SQL constraints

FDB26 · SQL command categories and exact scope

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

FDB16 · Write and enforce SQL constraints

FDB26 · SQL command categories and exact scope

Correct answer: A — UNIQUE

Uniqueness constrains distinct tuple identifiers, whereas SELECT, INSERT and DELETE are language operations in the DML list. UNIQUE is the offered constraint name matching that property.

Evidence in the complete study note

Example: CREATE TABLE Enrollment (StudentID INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES Student(StudentID), CourseID INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES Course(CourseID), Grade INTEGER CHECK (Grade BETWEEN 0 AND 100), PRIMARY KEY (StudentID,CourseID)); Each column declaration gives its name then type, with commas between declarations. CHECK is a constraint clause, not a standalone GRANT-like statement. POSITIVE is not a general SQL constraint keyword; CHECK (Amount > 0) expresses positivity.FDB16
DML retrieves or changes data: SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE.FDB26
Some texts separate SELECT as DQL.FDB26

Choice by choice

A · correct — Uniqueness is explicitly an integrity condition. (PDF pp. 14, 26.)

B · incorrect — SELECT is a retrieval command. (PDF pp. 14, 26.)

C · incorrect — INSERT is a manipulation command. (PDF pp. 14, 26.)

D · incorrect — DELETE is a manipulation command. (PDF pp. 14, 26.)

Referential integrity maintains consistency between related tuples by requiring non-null child references to resolve to existing parent keys. It controls valid relationships rather than authorization, performance, or encoding.

Referential integrity maintains consistency between related tuples by requiring non-null child references to resolve to existing parent keys. It controls valid relationships rather than authorization, performance, or encoding.

Referential integrity requires a non-NULL foreign-key value to match an eligible referenced unique/candidate key; self-references are allowed. A parent need not have a child.FDB15

Scalability, availability, fast retrieval, redundancy control and security are all reasonable design considerations.FDB38
