Study-guide references

FDB19 · Insertion, deletion and update anomalies

Correct answer: A — Reduce avoidable redundancy and associated integrity anomalies.

FDB19 defines normalization as separating facts according to their determinants, reducing avoidable repetition and insertion, deletion and update anomalies. That supports both parts of A. It does not promise universally faster queries, deliberately create complexity for its own sake or encrypt content. Performance and security need their own workload and threat-model choices.

Evidence in the complete study note

Suppose Enrollment repeats StudentName for every course. An update anomaly requires changing every copy of the student's name; missed copies disagree. An insertion anomaly prevents recording a new student until some enrollment exists. A deletion anomaly loses the only record of a student's details when their last enrollment is deleted. Revoke anomaly is not a normalization anomaly.FDB19
Normalization separates facts according to their determinants, reducing avoidable repetition and associated anomalies.FDB19
Adding a generated row ID may make insertion technically possible, but does not remove repeated facts or fix the dependency design.FDB19

Choice by choice

A · correct — Normalization reduces repeated facts and the anomalies that threaten data consistency.

B · incorrect — More joins may be needed, so faster query execution is not a universal normalization purpose.

C · incorrect — Relationships follow fact dependencies; creating complexity is not the goal.

D · incorrect — Normalization restructures facts and does not encrypt sensitive values.

Question 213

A weak entity uses its partial key together with the key of an independently keyed owner. Which terms describe that associated entity set? Select all that apply.

  • A. Identifying set
  • B. Strong entity set
  • C. Neighbor set
  • D. Owner set

Adapted from

  • Computer Science Exit Exam · 2018 · Question 12

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Check how a dependent entity obtains its complete identity.

Study-guide sections

FDB08 · Weak entities and identifying relationships

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

FDB08 · Weak entities and identifying relationships

Correct answers (select all): B, D — B. Strong entity set; D. Owner set

The weak entity lacks a complete independent identifier. Its owner's key combines with the partial key to identify it. Because the owner is independently keyed in this question, strong entity set and owner set both describe the associated set. Identifying describes the relationship connecting the sets, while neighbor does not state the necessary ownership and identification dependency.

Evidence in the complete study note

A weak entity lacks a complete independent key. Its owner is normally a strong entity, so owner entity set and strong entity set may both describe the associated set. Its partial key, also called discriminator, distinguishes weak entities of the same owner. Identification combines the owner key and partial key.FDB08
The weak entity participates totally in its identifying relationship.FDB08
Chen notation uses a double rectangle, double identifying diamond and dashed underline for a partial key.FDB08

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — Identifying is normally the relationship role rather than the defined name of the owner entity set.

B · correct — The owner that supplies an independent identifying key is normally a strong entity set.

C · incorrect — Neighbor does not express the necessary ownership and identification dependency.

D · correct — Owner set is the associated entity set whose key identifies the weak entity together with its partial key.

Question 214

A language that allows the DBA or user to describe and name the entities, attributes, and relationships required for the application is__________

  • A. Transaction control language (TCL)
  • B. Data control language (DCL)
  • C. Data Manipulation Language (DML)
  • D. Data Definition Language (DDL)

Source

  • Computer Science Exit Exam · 2018 · Question 14

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Classify each SQL command by the kind of change it performs.

Study-guide sections

FDB26 · SQL command categories and exact scope

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

FDB26 · SQL command categories and exact scope

Correct answer: D — Data Definition Language.

Describing and naming application entities, attributes and relationships defines schema structure. FDB26 assigns structural changes to DDL through CREATE, ALTER and DROP. TCL manages transaction boundaries, DCL controls privileges and DML retrieves or changes data. These categories have different effects, so DDL uniquely matches the schema-description role in the stem.

Evidence in the complete study note

DDL changes structure: CREATE, ALTER, DROP, commonly RENAME and TRUNCATE in textbook classifications. DML retrieves or changes data: SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE. Some texts separate SELECT as DQL. DCL manages privileges through GRANT and REVOKE. TCL includes COMMIT, ROLLBACK and SAVEPOINT. Classify a statement by its effect, not its length.FDB26

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — TCL manages commit, rollback and savepoints rather than defining schema elements.

B · incorrect — DCL manages privileges through GRANT and REVOKE rather than describing entities and attributes.

C · incorrect — DML retrieves or changes instance data rather than defining the application schema.

D · correct — DDL defines and changes schema structure, including tables, attributes and constraints.

Question 215

In relational-table terminology, which terms describe a unit of related field values treated together by an application? Select all that apply.

  • A. Rows
  • B. Field
  • C. Record
  • D. Database

Adapted from

  • Computer Science Exit Exam · 2018 · Question 20

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Distinguish a relation's structure from its current contents and its descriptive metadata.

Study-guide sections

FDB03 · Relations, schemas, instances and metadata

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

FDB03 · Relations, schemas, instances and metadata

Correct answers (select all): A, C — A. Rows; C. Record

A tuple holds a set of related attribute values as one table unit. Row and record are synonymous names for that unit, so both alternatives qualify. A field is only an individual property, and a database is the larger coherent collection of related data. Grammatical number in Rows does not create a different relational concept from Record.

Evidence in the complete study note

A relation is a set of tuples, represented as a table. A tuple/row/record contains related attribute values; an attribute/column/field describes a property. Degree counts columns; cardinality counts rows. Classical relations have distinct tuples, atomic cells and domains for attributes; row order has no meaning. Column order has no logical meaning when attributes are identified by name. SQL tables can contain duplicate rows unless constrained.FDB03

Choice by choice

A · correct — Rows contain related field values and are the relational counterpart of records.

B · incorrect — A field is an individual property rather than a collection of related fields.

C · correct — Record is the stated unit that groups related fields for application use.

D · incorrect — A database contains a larger coherent collection of data rather than just one record.

Question 216

Which template creates a table with one column? Treat table_name, column_name and column_type as placeholders for valid identifiers and a data type.

  • A. CREATE table_name (column_name, column type);
  • B. CREATE table_name (column_type, column name);
  • C. CREATE TABLE table_name (column_name column_type);
  • D. CREATE TABLE table_name (column_type, column name);

Adapted from

  • Computer Science Exit Exam · 2018 · Question 40

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Distinguish table definition changes from changes to stored rows.

Study-guide sections

FDB30 · CREATE and ALTER tables

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

FDB30 · CREATE and ALTER tables

Correct answer: C — C. CREATE TABLE table_name (column_name column_type);

CREATE TABLE introduces a new table definition. Within its parentheses, each column declaration places the column name before its data type, without a comma between those two parts. Commas separate whole declarations. Option C matches this pattern. A and B omit TABLE, while D reverses and separates the intended name/type pair and therefore fails the requested template.

Evidence in the complete study note

CREATE TABLE Employee (EmpID INTEGER PRIMARY KEY, Name VARCHAR(80)); declares two columns. The name and its type stay together; the comma separates whole column declarations. To add a primary key later: ALTER TABLE Employee ADD PRIMARY KEY (EmpID); existing data must be unique and non-NULL. A table cannot gain a second primary key without replacing the existing constraint.FDB30

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — TABLE is absent and the column declaration is split incorrectly.

B · incorrect — TABLE is absent and the name/type order is reversed.

C · correct — The name/type pair forms one complete column declaration.

D · incorrect — The type/name order and comma do not form the intended declaration.

Question 217

From the following alternatives which one is FALSE about database system?

  • A. It is very difficult to protect a file under the system
  • B. In database system the user is not required to write the procedures
  • C. In database system, DBMS provides a good protection mechanism.
  • D. It contains a wide variety of sophisticated techniques to store and retrieve the data.

Source

  • Computer Science Exit Exam · 2018 · Question 45

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Review the database approach and match each claimed benefit or cost to its mechanism.

Study-guide sections

FDB01 · Database approach, benefits and costs

FDB36 · Relational calculus: state the desired facts

FDB25 · Ordered, dense, sparse and multilevel indexes

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

FDB01 · Database approach, benefits and costs

FDB36 · Relational calculus: state the desired facts

FDB25 · Ordered, dense, sparse and multilevel indexes

Correct answer: A — The claim that protection is very difficult under a database system is false.

FDB01 contrasts unmanaged file difficulties with DBMS authorization, shared integrity, transactions and recovery. A therefore reverses a stated database benefit. B is understood in the data-access sense: declarative requests need not prescribe retrieval procedures, as FDB36 explains; it does not mean applications never contain code. Protection and sophisticated storage/access facilities in C and D are consistent with the note.

Evidence in the complete study note

A database is a coherent collection of related facts with meaning for an application. A DBMS is software that defines, stores, retrieves, updates, protects and shares that data. The database is the content; the DBMS manages it. A database system also includes applications, people and infrastructure.FDB01
Traditional file applications define their own formats and access procedures.FDB01
A DBMS centralizes metadata and supports multiple views, authorization, transactions, backup and recovery.FDB01

Choice by choice

A · correct — This reverses the note's benefit: a DBMS provides coordinated protection rather than inherently making protection very difficult.

B · incorrect — Declarative data requests need not prescribe retrieval procedures; this does not exclude ordinary application code.

C · incorrect — Authorization and controlled data access are explicit DBMS protection mechanisms.

D · incorrect — DBMS storage and access facilities include indexes, buffers, query processing and other sophisticated mechanisms.

Question 218

Consider a directed line(->) from the relationship set advisor to both entity sets instructor and student. This indicates _________ cardinality

  • A. Many to one
  • B. Many to many
  • C. One to one
  • D. One to many

Source

  • Computer Science Exit Exam · 2018 · Question 56

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Read the relationship in both directions and check participation constraints.

Study-guide sections

FDB09 · Cardinality, participation and direction

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

FDB09 · Cardinality, participation and direction

Correct answer: C — One-to-one under the common ER arrow convention.

FDB09 explicitly discusses arrows from advisor to both Instructor and Student. Under its common convention, each arrow imposes a maximum of one on the corresponding end. Both ends are therefore maximum-one, producing 1:1. This does not imply total participation, and another diagram notation would require a legend, but the complete note supplies the convention for this example.

Evidence in the complete study note

Read business rules in both directions. If each employee belongs to one department and each department has many employees, Employee→Department is N:1; Department→Employee is 1:N. Building→Location could also be N:1, so names alone cannot select a unique example. Under the common arrow convention, arrows from advisor to both Instructor and Student impose maximum-one at both ends, giving 1:1. Without a notation convention, arrows are unsafe evidence.FDB09

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — Many-to-one permits many at one end, contrary to maximum-one arrows at both ends.

B · incorrect — Many-to-many imposes neither of the maximum-one constraints stated by the arrows.

C · correct — Maximum one at both ends produces a one-to-one relationship.

D · incorrect — One-to-many permits multiple associations at one end rather than maximum one at both.

Question 219

What is the basic goal of Normalization?

  • A. To decrease data integrity
  • B. To reduce redundancy
  • C. To maximize transitive dependency
  • D. To increase dependency

Source

  • Computer Science Exit Exam · 2018 · Question 70

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Distinguish dependency-based design problems from the contents of one sample table.

Study-guide sections

FDB19 · Insertion, deletion and update anomalies

FDB21 · Third normal form, BCNF and higher forms

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

FDB19 · Insertion, deletion and update anomalies

FDB21 · Third normal form, BCNF and higher forms

Correct answer: B — Reduce avoidable redundancy.

Normalization separates facts by their determinants to reduce repeated storage and the anomalies that repetition can cause. FDB19 therefore supports reducing redundancy as the basic listed goal. Decreasing integrity is the opposite intended effect, and maximizing transitive or unspecified dependencies is not a normalization objective. Proper dependencies are analyzed rather than increased indiscriminately.

Evidence in the complete study note

Suppose Enrollment repeats StudentName for every course. An update anomaly requires changing every copy of the student's name; missed copies disagree. An insertion anomaly prevents recording a new student until some enrollment exists. A deletion anomaly loses the only record of a student's details when their last enrollment is deleted. Revoke anomaly is not a normalization anomaly.FDB19
Normalization separates facts according to their determinants, reducing avoidable repetition and associated anomalies.FDB19
Remove dependencies of nonprime facts on nonkey determinants.FDB21

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — Normalization aims to improve integrity by reducing anomalies, not decrease it.

B · correct — Reducing avoidable repeated facts is a basic normalization goal.

C · incorrect — Problematic nonkey transitive dependencies are removed or separated rather than maximized.

D · incorrect — Dependencies are analyzed to place facts correctly; increasing them indiscriminately is not the objective.

Question 220

For a relation with ID, CITY and NAME, ID is guaranteed unique and non-NULL while CITY and NAME may repeat. Which listed sets are superkeys? Select all that apply.

  • A. NAME
  • B. ID
  • C. CITY
  • D. CITY, ID

Adapted from

  • Computer Science Exit Exam · 2018 · Question 77

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Test uniqueness and minimality separately when classifying keys.

Study-guide sections

FDB07 · Superkeys, candidate keys and primary keys

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

FDB07 · Superkeys, candidate keys and primary keys

Correct answers (select all): B, D — B. ID; D. CITY, ID

A superkey is any set of attributes guaranteed to identify a tuple. ID has that guarantee, and the larger set containing CITY and ID keeps it. Both sets therefore qualify, even though the larger one is not minimal. CITY and NAME may repeat and cannot independently identify all tuples. Minimality would distinguish candidate keys, but it is not required for superkeys.

Evidence in the complete study note

A superkey uniquely identifies a tuple. A candidate key is a minimal superkey: removing any component loses uniqueness. Minimal means no unnecessary attribute, not necessarily the fewest columns among all keys. A primary key is one selected candidate key; remaining candidates are alternate keys. A composite key contains multiple attributes and need not be the chosen primary key.FDB07
If ID is unique, both {ID} and {ID,City} are superkeys, but only {ID} is minimal.FDB07
A table can have several candidates and foreign keys but at most one declared primary-key constraint, possibly containing several columns.FDB07

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — Repeated names are permitted, so NAME is not guaranteed unique.

B · correct — ID uniquely identifies every tuple.

C · incorrect — Repeated cities are permitted, so CITY is not guaranteed unique.

D · correct — Including unique ID preserves identification even with the extra CITY attribute.

Question 221

______________is one of the candidate keys chosen by the database designer to uniquely identify the entity set.

  • A. Super key
  • B. Candidate key
  • C. Social security key
  • D. Primary key

Source

  • Computer Science Exit Exam · 2018 · Question 95

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Test uniqueness and minimality separately when classifying keys.

Study-guide sections

FDB07 · Superkeys, candidate keys and primary keys

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

FDB07 · Superkeys, candidate keys and primary keys

Correct answer: D — Primary key.

FDB07 defines a primary key as the selected candidate key, with the remaining candidates called alternate keys. The stem asks specifically for the designer's selection rather than every unique attribute set. Superkey is broader and candidate key denotes eligibility before selection. A social-security identifier could be chosen, but is not the generic name of that role.

Evidence in the complete study note

A superkey uniquely identifies a tuple. A candidate key is a minimal superkey: removing any component loses uniqueness. Minimal means no unnecessary attribute, not necessarily the fewest columns among all keys. A primary key is one selected candidate key; remaining candidates are alternate keys. A composite key contains multiple attributes and need not be the chosen primary key.FDB07

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — A superkey is any unique identifier set and may include unnecessary attributes.

B · incorrect — Candidate keys are eligible minimal identifiers, not specifically the one selected for the primary role.

C · incorrect — A social-security identifier is a possible attribute choice, not the generic selected-key category.

D · correct — The selected candidate key is the primary key.

Question 222

What is the difference between a primary key and a foreign key?

    Source

    • Database Fundamentals review questions · Question 1

    Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

    Test uniqueness and minimality separately when classifying keys.

    Study-guide sections

    FDB07 · Superkeys, candidate keys and primary keys

    FDB15 · Integrity and constraint taxonomy

    FDB16 · Write and enforce SQL constraints

    Answer & Detailed Explanation

    Study-guide references

    FDB07 · Superkeys, candidate keys and primary keys

    FDB15 · Integrity and constraint taxonomy

    FDB16 · Write and enforce SQL constraints

    Answer: A primary key is the selected unique non-NULL identifier; a foreign key references an eligible key.

    FDB07 defines the primary key as a selected candidate key. FDB15 requires non-NULL foreign-key values to match an eligible referenced unique/candidate key; foreign keys need not be unique and may permit NULL.

    Evidence in the complete study note

    A superkey uniquely identifies a tuple. A candidate key is a minimal superkey: removing any component loses uniqueness. Minimal means no unnecessary attribute, not necessarily the fewest columns among all keys. A primary key is one selected candidate key; remaining candidates are alternate keys. A composite key contains multiple attributes and need not be the chosen primary key.FDB07
    Referential integrity requires a non-NULL foreign-key value to match an eligible referenced unique/candidate key; self-references are allowed.FDB15
    Entity integrity requires every primary-key component to be non-NULL.FDB15

    Question 223

    Why is database normalization important? What problems does it solve?

      Source

      • Database Fundamentals review questions · Question 2

      Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

      Distinguish dependency-based design problems from the contents of one sample table.

      Study-guide sections

      FDB19 · Insertion, deletion and update anomalies

      Answer & Detailed Explanation

      Study-guide references

      FDB19 · Insertion, deletion and update anomalies

      Answer: Normalization reduces repeated facts and insertion, deletion and update anomalies.

      Repeating a student name across enrollments risks inconsistent updates, prevents storing some facts without unrelated rows and can lose a fact when its last repeated row is deleted. Normalization separates facts by their determinants to reduce these anomalies.

      Evidence in the complete study note

      Suppose Enrollment repeats StudentName for every course. An update anomaly requires changing every copy of the student's name; missed copies disagree. An insertion anomaly prevents recording a new student until some enrollment exists. A deletion anomaly loses the only record of a student's details when their last enrollment is deleted. Revoke anomaly is not a normalization anomaly.FDB19
      Normalization separates facts according to their determinants, reducing avoidable repetition and associated anomalies.FDB19
      Adding a generated row ID may make insertion technically possible, but does not remove repeated facts or fix the dependency design.FDB19

      Question 224

      What happens if you UPDATE a row but forget to include a WHERE clause?

        Source

        • Database Fundamentals review questions · Question 3

        Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

        Track which rows each data-changing command affects.

        Study-guide sections

        FDB29 · INSERT, UPDATE and DELETE

        FDB16 · Write and enforce SQL constraints

        Answer & Detailed Explanation

        Study-guide references

        FDB29 · INSERT, UPDATE and DELETE

        FDB16 · Write and enforce SQL constraints

        Answer: UPDATE without WHERE targets every row, subject to constraints and transaction outcome.

        FDB29 states that an UPDATE without WHERE targets every row. The SET expressions are therefore applied to all rows rather than one intended row; constraints or errors can still prevent completion.

        Evidence in the complete study note

        INSERT INTO Hotel(HotelID,HotelName,SubCity) VALUES (1,'Hilton','Yeka'); pairs each named column with a compatible value. Counts and types must match; keys and other constraints still apply. Without a column list, values must follow the table's declared column order.FDB29
        UPDATE without WHERE targets every row.FDB29
        UPDATE Book SET Price = 600 WHERE ID = 'B001'; changes matching rows.FDB29

        Question 225

        Why would you use a ROLLBACK command in a transaction?

          Source

          • Database Fundamentals review questions · Question 4

          Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

          Match each transaction guarantee to its precise failure or interference scenario.

          Study-guide sections

          FDB37 · Transactions and the ACID properties

          Answer & Detailed Explanation

          Study-guide references

          FDB37 · Transactions and the ACID properties

          Answer: ROLLBACK undoes uncommitted transaction work, for example after a failure or unwanted change.

          FDB37 contrasts COMMIT, which completes transaction work, with ROLLBACK, which undoes uncommitted work. Rollback restores the prior transactional state after errors or a decision not to keep the changes; it does not erase other users' committed transactions.

          Evidence in the complete study note

          For a transfer, subtracting 100 from one balance and adding 100 to another must commit together; losing either half violates atomicity and may violate the total-balance invariant. BEGIN starts work, COMMIT completes it, ROLLBACK undoes uncommitted work and SAVEPOINT marks an intermediate rollback point. A failed transaction may roll back while other users' committed transactions remain. SQL transaction syntax and error-handling constructs differ by DBMS; @@ERROR/IF is SQL Server-specific.FDB37

          Question 226

          What is the difference between DELETE and DROP?

            Source

            • Database Fundamentals review questions · Question 5

            Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

            Classify each SQL command by the kind of change it performs.

            Study-guide sections

            FDB26 · SQL command categories and exact scope

            FDB29 · INSERT, UPDATE and DELETE

            Answer & Detailed Explanation

            Study-guide references

            FDB26 · SQL command categories and exact scope

            FDB29 · INSERT, UPDATE and DELETE

            Answer: DELETE removes rows and retains the table; DROP TABLE removes the table object.

            FDB26 distinguishes data deletion from structural removal. DELETE may target selected rows with WHERE, or all rows without it, while leaving the table defined. DROP TABLE removes that relation object.

            Evidence in the complete study note

            DELETE removes rows and keeps the table; DROP TABLE removes the table object; ALTER changes its definition. TRUNCATE empties a table using implementation-specific mechanisms. PostgreSQL allows transactional rollback of TRUNCATE, so no rollback information is retained is not a universal SQL property. SQL Server permits DROP DATABASE Sales, suppliers; that comma-list syntax is vendor-specific. SQLite's .databases lists attached names/files in its command-line shell; .database is not the documented command.FDB26
            UPDATE without WHERE targets every row.FDB29
            DELETE FROM r; removes every row but leaves r defined.FDB29
